2187. THOMAS BAYLIS was indicted for embezzlement.
SAMUEL WESTCOTT TILKE. I live in High-street, Mary-le-bone, and am a baker. The prisoner was my apprentice, and employed to receive money; George Parker owed me 3s. 9d.
GEORGE PARKER. I paid the prisoner 3s. 9d. on the 7th of October, on Mr. Tilke's account.
MR. TILKE. He paid me 11d. on this account, on the 7th of October; here is the entry in his own writing - he settles with me every evening; he was taken on another charge.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was it not for spoiling rolls that you took him? A. Yes, on the 7th of October, in the evening; but he had made up his book before he went; if there was any errors we correct them on Sunday - he once summoned me to the office: the Magistrate dismissed the charge, and said it was a mercy to horsewhip him, for kicking me; I said if he went on in that way he would soon be there again - he has been two years with me.
Prisoner's Defence. The very evening I made this mistake I pointed out an error of five loaves.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined Three Months.

2188. HENRY EDWARDS was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. BODKIN conducted the prosecution.
CATHERINE EDWARDS. I am the wife of Henry Edwards, who left me about eleven years ago; I have not heard of him for eight years. The prisoner is my eldest son; I am a baker - he managed my business for some years, and conducted himself well till lately. On the 13th of October Biggs owed me 13l.
MARGARET BIGGS. I keep a chandler's-shop, and deal with the prosecutrix. On the 13th of October I owed her 13l. 0s. 1d.; the prisoner came that day, and said."I want some money - my mother has a bill to pay for flour;" I gave him ten sovereigns, and he gave me this receipt - (reads.)
MRS. EDWARDS. He never returned to me after this; I have not had the money.
JAMES HENRY BRYNE. I keep a chandler's-shop, and know the prosecutrix. I found the prisoner in Old-street; I took hold of him, and he said the money was all gone - I delivered him to an officer.
HENRY SHELDRAKE. I am an officer. I took the prisoner, and found on him 17s. 4d.; he said that was all, but I found a bag with five sovereigns on him after that.
Prisoner's Defence. I think it useless to deny my guilt - I hope judgement will be passed with mercy.
GUILTY. Aged 22.
Transported for Fourteen Years.

2189. JOHN EDWARDS was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. BODKIN conducted the prosecution.
CATHERINE EDWARDS. I am a baker; my husband left me eleven years ago - I do not know whether he is dead or alive. The prisoner is my son - he is twenty years of age; he was employed as my servant in business - I had a customer named James Hook, who owed me 1l. 3s. 4d.; the prisoner used to serve him - he accounted to me only for 13s. 4d. received from Mr. Hook, and said he had spent 10s. at skittles; he said it was a good thing he had saved that.
JOHN HOOK. On the 17th of September the prisoner called on me, and I paid him 1l. 3s. 4d. on account of his mother.
WILLIAM HALL. I took the prisoner; he was quite intoxicated - in taking him to the office he said he did not know what I took him for, unless it was disorderly conduct; I said it was for receiving 10s. - I said, "I don't wish to know any thing about it, but it is right you should know before you go before the Magistrate."
Prisoner's Defence (written).
On the 1st of last month I received warning to quit my mother's house, but owing to my not being able to get employment, she consented to let me stop until I could get into a situation. On the following Wednesday I went to the house of Mr. Hook with bread, who (without being asked), paid me 1l. 3s. 4d. as part of his bill; and on my leaving his house I met with some friends, and having no money of my own, I spent 10s., and of course gave my mother but 13s. 4d., she agreeing that she would stop it out of my week's wages; but on Monday she paid me my full money, telling me that she would place it to an account she had against me of monies paid by her on my account.
COURT to CATHERINE EDWARDS. Q. Why did you not charge him with this till October? A. Because I wanted to keep him in my house, but his conduct was such I could not endure it; I never gave him permission to take out money, but he has done it; I would have placed this to his account, but his conduct was such; he has put me in fear of my life - I have been forced to have an officer in the house to keep me from blows and insults - I did say I would place it to account that night, but my little son told me that he was just as usual, very drunk - I have devised every means to reclaim him.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury.
Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18281023-257]
2190. GEORGE BRISCALL and FREDERICK CRAWKSHAW were indicted for stealing, on the 15th of October, 9 lbs. weight of beef, value 2s., the goods of John Day.
JOHN DAY. I am a butcher, and live at Fulham. On the 15th of October I was out; when I came home this beef was missing - I firmly believe it to have been mine.
JOHN TOUSE. I am a gardener. On the 15th of October I saw Crawkshaw take a piece of beef from the prosecutor's shop-board, and put it into Briscall's apron.
ELISHA GIBBS. I was going down a lane which leads to Shepherd's-bush; I saw the prisoners - one of them had a piece of beef in his apron; I ran up to them - they dropped the beef, and I pursued and took them; I showed the same beef to the prosecutor.
WILLIAM SMITH. I took the prisoners, and found where the beef had been in their apron.
CRAWKSHAW'S Defence. When he heard the cry of Stop thief! he took Briscall, and said, "Throw it over the pales," but he would not - he then went on, but he ran and took him, when he heard there was 5s. reward offered.
ELISHA GIBBS. I knew of no reward at that time.
BRISCALL - GUILTY. Aged 18.
CRAWKSHAW - GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined Six Months.

2198. ROBERT BUTLER was indicted for stealing, on the 16 of October, 1 fixture, (i.e.) 1 copper, value 10s., the goods of Francis Whit, and fixed to a certain building; 1 blanket, value 1s.; 1 apron, value 2d., and 1 knife, value 6d., the goods of Hannah Roe; 1 bonnet value 6d.; 1 piece of crape, value 2d.; 1 handkerchief value 2d.; 1 apron, value 2d.; 1 pair of gloves, value 6d., and 2 caps, value 6d., the goods of Jane Nash.
HANNAH ROE. I had the care of a house at the corner of St. Martin's-lane and Newport-street; I left it on the 16th of October, at a quarter-past five o'clock in the evening; the property was then all safe - the prisoner was taken about half-past seven o'clock: I did not hear of it till the following morning, when I went to the house, and the things were gone; the blanket, apron, and knife belonged to me, and had been in the back parlour - there were some other articles of Jane Nash's; these are the articles - I know nothing of the prisoner.
JANE NASH. There were some things of mine in the house that evening, but I had not seen them for three weeks or a month - these are them.
GEORGE BOWER. I saw the prisoner on the 16th of October, with a copper on his head, in Gray's Inn-lane - I asked where he got it; he said from No. 12, Tottenham-street - I was not satisfied, and left him with my brother officer; I went to the place he stated, and found it was not true; my brother officer found the other things on him; I fitted the copper in the house - it fitted exactly.
GEORGE FOSTER. I was with Bower. I found these other articles on the prisoner, four picklock-keys, a phosphorus-box, and a screw-driver - they were all in his pockets.
JOHN WILLIAMS. I am porter to Mr. Way - he is a grocer. I open and shut the house in question; I went on the morning of the 16th of October, and found the door half open; I went in and found no one - I went and told Mrs. Roe - we went in, and missed the articles.
Prisoner's Defence. I met a person in Rupert-street, who asked me to carry this copper over the water for him- the other things he said he brought from a lodging in Tottenham-street; I do not know the number - I thought it was 12.
GUILTY. Aged 28.
Transported for Seven Years.

WILLIAM GRIMES, JUN. JOHN BEE. I am an officer. I took the prisoner in the City-road; I asked him about the trousers - he said they were at his mother's, and she had got them safe; I went there, and found them.

2195. MARIA WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of October, 4 table-cloths, value 5s., the goods of Henry Millman.
HENRY MILLMAN. I am a chop-house keeper, and live in Brydges-street. On the 19th of October I lost four tablecloths from my dining-room - I was standing in front of my house, and a young man said to me "What business has this woman in the passage?" - I looked and saw the prisoner with the four cloths on her arm; I went to her, and she said I might take them, and let her go.
THOMAS MORTON. I saw the prisoner on the staircase with the cloths on her arm - I went into the yard, and something struck me that all was not right; I turned back, and told the prosecutor.
JOHN EAMES. I took the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I never had them in my hand - I merely walked in and out again; he pushed me out, and I said "Don't hurt me."

[trialAccount t18281023-263]
2196. NICHOLAS SCOTT was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of October, 1 watch, value 3l.; 2 seals, value 2l.; 1 ring, value 10s., and 1 watch-key, value 10s,, the goods of William Henry Taylor.
WILLIAM HENRY TAYLOR. I lost a watch, seals, and a key, on the 20th of October, from the loft of the building of Mr. Robert Taylor 's, in whose service I am - I left it safe in my chest, when I went on the top of the building for about an hour, and when I returned, it was gone - the prisoner was in Mr. Taylor's employ.
Cross-examined by MR. MILLER. Q. How long has he worked in Mr. Taylor's employ? A. About three years, and had a very good character - he had 10s. or 12s. a week.
HERBERT JOHN CLARK. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Wardour-street - this watch was pawned with me on Monday, the 20th of October, between one and three o'clock, by the prisoner, in the name of William Scott; I am confident it was him, because he came a second time the same day, after we had closed business; and said he had lost the duplicate of the watch, and wished me to detain it, if it were brought to me - I would say nothing that night; the next day the prosecutor came and said he had received a letter, saying it was pawned at our house.
Cross-examined. Q. What is the value of it? A. It is worth altogether about 7l.; I lent him 2s. 6d., which was all he asked - had he asked more, it would have aroused my suspicions; I cannot fix the time more accurately than between one and three o'clock - I was in the shop all day, and may have had two hundred customers; but this was a remarkable case, it being pawned for 2s. 6d. it excited my attention.
Q. Can you call to mind your recollection of his features? A. His asking 2s. 6d. lulled my suspicion; I have said excited my attention - he came the second time between eight and nine o'clock in the evening; I have always named that hour, and never said it was between nine and ten - Mr. Harrison, my master, said that; I heard the prisoner say at the office he would account for where he was between nine and ten o'clock; nobody said "Then we must provide for that, or any thing like it;" I said it was between eight and nine - I went to Taylor and Beaumont's factory to point him out; he was the third man who came out, I recognized him by his countenance and dress - he had a blue coat and corderoy trowsers; there were ten or fifteen men - he came into the box when he pawned it, and I saw him as he went out at the door; I have not advised him to plead guilty - he was advised, but said he was not guilty, that he never saw me nor the watch; when he came at night, he came to the private door, and there was a very strong light over the door.
Q. Was Mr. Harrison present when the prisoner came a second time? A. No; I saw his brother at the office, and he is here - I have no doubt of the prisoner being the person.
MR. MILLER called -
HENRY GREEN. I am a harness-maker, and work at Messrs. Taylor and Beaumont's factory. I know the prisoner, and recollect his being at the shop on Monday week; he was in the shop till one o'clock, when he went to dinner - he dines in Red Lion-street, Old Cavendish-street; the shop is in Brook-street, Bond-street - I saw himthere again when I returned, at five minutes before two - I do not know the precise time he returned; he complained of his head aching that afternoon - he staid till five o'clock and went to tea; I returned at half-past five, and found him in the shop then - he worked till eight o'clock - we went together across the square to Regent-street, and then down Oxford-street to the Middlesex-hospital; he turned to Tottenham-court-road, and I wished him good night; that was his road home - he lives with his father in Camden-town; it would take him about twenty minutes to go to his father's - he is very steady in his habits; I never was in the loft the watch was taken from; I know nothing of its being taken - I was not present when my master urged the prisoner to acknowledge having taken it; several of our men wear blue coats and corderoy trousers - I know nothing about an anonymous letter.
- SUPTON. The prisoner works under me at the shop; I recollect his being there on Monday week - he left at one o'clock, and told me he returned at half-past - I saw him again three minutes before two; I did not see him asleep - he is an honest hard-working lad; the loft the watch was in, is an open loft - trades-people pass through it.
ROBERT SHORT. I work in the same shop; I recollect seeing the prisoner on Monday week; he returned from dinner at twenty-five minutes past one o'clock - he goes to Old Cavendish-street with his father to dine, as it is too far to go to Camden-town; I should think it would take him half an hour to go to Cavendish-street, eat his dinner, and return; I think I may venture to say he could not have gone to Wardour-street and eaten his dinner and returned; I heard no offer made to him about this.
COURT. Q. What day was it that he returned at twenty-five minutes past one o'clock? A. Last Monday week; it is my duty to attend the front-shop while the men go to dinner, to watch when they return - I have my dinner at two o'clock; two other men came between one and two - they were Green and Burn; Green came first of these two - there was no particular reason why I should notice the time the prisoner returned; but he generally does come about that time - he never takes his full hour, not one time in ten.
- SCOTT. I am the prisoner's father, and work for the same master, but not in the same factory. On Monday week I recollect his dining with me and my other son; we all dine together regularly - it takes us about twenty minutes to dine; I did not notice the time he left that day, but I think it wanted about five minutes of the half hour when he went away - he, being the youngest, generally puts away the dinner things; I recollect his being at home to tea at half-past five o'clock; and at night I went home a quarter-past nine, and he was at home before me - my wife said he had been in bed with a head-ache half an hour; I will take an oath he did not go out after I went home - he could not without my knowing it; I heard of the circumstance, and I said to him, "If you go to the pawnbroker and he swears to you, you know you will be prosecuted; but if you are guilty you have no occasion to go, the master won't prosecute you" - he said, "No, I am not guilty; I would sooner do any thing than confess a thing I am not guilty of."
JOHN SCOTT. I am the prisoner's brother. I recollect his dining with me and my father that day - he went away about the usual time; no sooner than usual - when I went home at night he was in bed.
MARY ANN SCOTT. I was at home at my father's on Monday week. The prisoner came home about twenty minutes to nine o'clock - he went to bed with a very bad head-ache, he had no supper - he continued in bed till my father came home; I suppose my father's house is half an hour's walk from Wardour-street.
- SUPTON. I remember his having a bad headache that afternoon; when he returned he could not do his work - I finished it for him.
MR. BEAUMONT. I am one of the proprietors of the factory. The prisoner has worked there four years - he is very sober, honest, and industrious; the pawnbroker came and picked him out - I pointed out the consequences to him, and told him if he were guilty to own it, and Mr. Taylor would not prosecute him, and we would employ him still; he said he was innocent.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281023-264]
2197. JAMES WATSON was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of October, 1 coat, value 40s., the goods of William Wiggins.
WILLIAM WIGGINS. I lost a coat on the 1st of October, from Exmouth-street, Spafields. Martha Richards gave it to the prisoner; I knew him by working for my father - I had seen the coat safe at nine o'clock that morning in my drawer.
MARTHA RICHARDS. I was at the prosecutor's house on the 1st of October, when the prisoner came and asked for a dark green great coat for Mr. Wiggins, which I gave him; he did not say where it was, but I knew, and gave it him.
SIMON GILES. This coat was brought to me for sale on the 30th of September, by a young man resembling the prisoner - I never saw him before: I believe it to be him - I bought it for 18s.; he told me it was not his own, but a gentleman's, who had sent him, and if he did not approve of the price he should return before four o'clock.
GEORGE WADDINGTON. The prisoner was given into my custody on the evening of the 30th of September - he told me he had been sent by two other persons for the coat, and sold it at Mr. Giles - I went there, and got it.
COURT to WILLIAM WIGGINS. Q. What day was it? A. I think it was the 1st of October.
MARTHA RICHARDS. I think it was the 1st of October.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. It is useless for me to make any defence - I am guilty. I had been out of employ five weeks, and was led into this by the prosecutor's own brother; I sold it to Mr. Giles, and the reason I made that agreement was to know if his brother would be satisfied.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury and Prosecutor, believing him to have been in distress.
Confined Two Months.

2198. CHARLES LANGLEY & CLARA KIMPTON were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of September, 6 shirts, value 19s.; 4 table-cloths, value 16s., and 4 towels, value 4s., the goods of Philip Adams.
SECOND COUNT, stealing on the same day, 1 tablecloth, value 12s., the goods of Ann Ricketts; and 1 shirt, value 5s., the goods of William Murray .
ANN ADAMS. I am the wife of Philip Adams - he lives in Portland-town; I take in washing. On the 24th of September I had a large basket of linen in my yard; the shirts, table-cloths, and towels, stated in the indictment, were among them - there is one table-cloth and 1 shirt here now, which were in the basket; I cannot say when they were safe, but I heard an alarm about ten minutes before seven o'clock in the evening.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I suppose you are married? A. Yes, Sir, and no one has a right to doubt it: I am living with my husband - my name is not Long; I have never gone by that name, nor do I know a person of that name - I never was married to a person named Long.
MARY WARDELL. I live in Portland-terrace. This table-cloth belongs to my daughter, Ann Rickett's; she is a widow, and sends her things to Mrs. Adams to wash.
FREDERICK HICKS. I am a pawnbroker. I have a shirt, pawned with me on the 27th of September, by a woman - I do not know who, in the name of Harriet Jenkins.
RICHARD HARTLEY WALL. I am an officer. In consequence of information, I went on the 13th of October, to No. 2, Little Church-street, where the prisoners were living as man and wife: I found this table-cloth in the room - Kimpton said there was nothing there, but what her mother had given her, and she had bought; the table-cloth was in the bed, but I did not take it then - I went again and took it.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you looked at it the first time? A. Yes, and I thought it was a sheet; I returned in the evening and took it - it was not removed.
PHILIP WEBSTER. I was with Wall; I received from Langley's hand this purse, containing eleven duplicates, and one of them is for this shirt, pawned at Smith's, in Edgware-road, on the 27th of September, in the name of Harriet Jenkins.(Property produced and sworn to.)
KIMPTON'S Defence. I had the tickets given to me by James Brown, who was going to Edgware, and I said I would take care of them.
LANGLEY - GUILTY. Aged 21.
KIMPTON - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Seven Years.

JOHN KING. OLD COURT.
TUESDAY, OCTOBER 28th.
THE KING against JOSEPH HUNTON.
The prisoner having challenged the regular Jurors, the following Gentlemen were sworn: -
Solomon Rogers
Thomas Hilson Giles
William Taylor
William Cobley
James Barnard
John Middleton
James Robson
Lewelyn Watkin Williams
William Sutton
Thomas Harding
George Hazlewood Worral
Thomas Hill Sayer
Before Mr. Justice Park.
2202. JOSEPH HUNTON was indicted for that he, on the 1st of August, at St. Mary-le-Bow, having in his custody and possession, a certain Bill of Exchange, which is as follows: -
No.228. £162 9s. 0d. LONDON, 24h July 1928.
Three Months after Date, pay to our Order, One Hundred and Sixty-two Pounds, Nine Shillings, Value received, JOHN DICKSON & Co.
To Henry Mountain, Bury St. Edmunds, Suffolk. upon which said bill of exchange, was written a certainfalse, forged, and counterfeited acceptance of the said bill of exchange, which is a follows: -
Accepted and payable at Messrs. Lees & Co., Bankers, London,
HY. MOUNTAIN.
he, the said Joseph Hunton, afterwards, (to wit) on the same day, at the same parish, feloniously did utter and publish as true, the said false, forged, and counterfeited acceptance, of the said bill of exchange, he well knowing the same to be false, forged, and counterfeited, with intention to defraud Sir William Curtis, Bart., and others; against the Statute, &c.
ANOTHER COUNT, the same as the former, only for offering, disposing of, and putting away, the said forged acceptance, instead of for uttering and publishing it as true.
TWO OTHER COUNTS. like the former two, only with intent to defraud John Dickson.
TWO OTHER COUNTS, like the former, only with intent to defraud Henry Mountain.
MESSRS. BRODRICK & LAW conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM CURTIS. ESQ. I am a partner in the house of Sir William Curtis and Co.; Mr. Abraham Wildey Robarts is the other partner. I know the house of John Dickson and Co.; the firm was composed of John Dickson and the prisoner Joseph Hunton; their business was carried on in Ironmonger-lane; they were warehousemen - they kept an account at our house, as their bankers.
Q. Were you personally in the habit of receiving bills of exchange from Dickson or the prisoner? A. From the prisoner only; we received them from him for the purpose of being discounted. On the 1st of August, I saw the prisoner at our banking-house, in Lombard-street, in the City - he brought various bills of exchange with him for the purpose of being discounted.
Q. Look at this bill (producing one) and state whether it was among the bills produced to you on the 1st of August by the prisoner? A. Yes, it was.
Q. Did you put any question to the prisoner on the subject of that bill? A. I had generally a great deal of conversation with him respecting various bills which he offered for discount - I even hesitated to discount them at all, and I desired him to explain the nature of the various bills which he brought to me, as they were upon persons with whom I could have no acquaintance; he replied that they were bills drawn by his house on the various parties in the country, with whom they had transactions, and if houses in London refused to discount that paper, they could not carry on their business; I called his attention to this particular bill, it being among the rest - I asked him who Mountain was - he said he was a person in business, with whom his house had transactions.
Q. After you had gone through the several bills produced to you, what did you do with them? A. I took them into our discount-office and placed them in a box, which is appropriated exclusively to receive paper of that description; these bills would in the regular course of business, be carried through the various books belonging to the establishment that day; they would be first entered in the discount-book by Mr. Brackett, our clerk; the entries would all be made on that day, the 1st of August - after being entered in the discount-book they would be entered in the bill-book by Mr. Clunie, who had that department on that day; the bills are then tied together, and delivered over to the clerk who has the care of the bills of the house.
COURT. Q. When you discount bills, do you give the money for them, or place it to the account of the person? A. It is placed to their account.
THOMAS BRACKETT. In August last, I was a clerk in Messrs. Curtis' house - it was my duty to enter in the discount-book bills which were discounted every day; here is the discount book (producing it) - I have entered here on the 1st of August, a bill for 162l. 9s. drawn by John Dixon and Co. - the entry was made by myself on the 1st of August.
Q. Look at this bill, and tell us if you made the entry from that? A. I did - it is the same bill; after making the entry, I put the bills on the top of my desk, under a lead, and another clerk takes the same bills away to enter them in the bill-book; this entry in my book puts the bills to the credit of the party - here is the entry; this bill of 162l.9s. entered to the credit of John Dickson and Co., the house of which the prisoner is a partner; only the amount and the date when due are entered; it was due on the 27th of October, (yesterday) - I presented it for payment myself at Messrs. Lees and Co., the bankers, where it is made payable - it was not paid.
ALEXANDER CLUNIE. I am clerk to Sir William Curtis and Co. On the 1st of August, I received from Mr. Brackett's desk some bills to be entered in the bill-book, in the regular course of business - the bill produced was one of them; I entered it in the bill-book - here is the entry -(reads); John Dickson and Co. - Mountain (the acceptor) 24 - 7. (the date) three months (being the date at which it is drawn) - 27 - 10 (the date when due) I have put D against it, which means discounted; this entry is all my hand-writing; it is my habit to write on the bill the letter and folio of the book it is entered in; and I have written on this bill D 588; I made the entry on the day the bills came into our possession.
JOHN DICKSON. I was in partnership with the prisoner this year - our firm was John Dickson and Co.; Joseph Hunton attended to the drawing and discounting of bills.
Q. Were books kept by him in which entries of discount were made? A. Not any that I am aware of - not in our ware house; the body of this bill is in the prisoners handwriting, and also the name of the person on whom it is drawn; I believe it to be so - I have often seen him write.
Q. Had you any customer that you knew of, of the name of Henry Mountain, of Bury St. Edmunds, Suffolk? A. Not that I am aware of (looking at a letter, document No. 1) - I believe this to be Joseph Hunton's hand-writing, and this also - (No. 2.)
WILLIAM JAMES. I am ledger-clerk at Messrs. Lees and Co's. banking-house, Lombard-street. Henry Mountain has no account with that house; I know this bill was presented for payment yesterday - no such person keeps an account at Messrs. Lees - there is no other banker of their name.
HENRY MOUNTAIN. I live at Bury St. Edmunds, and am acquainted with the prisoner; I was in his employment about nine months back, as a draper , at Bury St. Edmunds - he at that time carried on business there as a draper; Ilived with him, as an apprentice for six years - I am now twenty-one years old, and have lived at Bury all my life- I know of no other Henry Mountain there; I am now shopman to a draper at Bury St. Edmunds - not in business on my own account; I never had any dealings with the house of Dickson and Co. - Mr. Dickson was in partnership with Mr. Hunton at the time I was apprentice to him- I had never any business transactions with them, and was not indebted to them any money; I never bought any goods of them - the acceptance on this bill is not my hand-writing (looking at it) nor any part of it, nor was it done by my authority.
COURT. Q. Do you sign your name Hy? A. No - only with an H.
JOSEPH WARREN. I am shopman to a draper at Bury. I know the last witness - I live in the same service as him, and know his hand-writing (looking at the acceptance on the bill) - I do not believe this to be his hand-writing; I know no other Henry Mountain at Bury - I am well acquainted with Bury, and with Mountain's handwriting.
COURT. Q. What opportunities have you had of seeing him write? A. From living six years with him when in the prisoner's employ - I saw him write very frequently.
ABRAHAM WILDEY ROBARTS. ESQ. I am one of the firm of Sir William Curtis and Co. The prisoner applied to me to discount some bills, which I declined; I cannot recollect the precise date, but it was either on the Wednesday or Thursday preceding the date on which I received this letter(looking at document No. 1). I received this by post - it is addressed to me as M. P. but is above number, and the postage was charged; I received it on the Monday subsequent to the Wednesday on which I refused to discount bills tor him - it has the Deal post-mark on it (read).
No. 1. - To A. W. Robarts, Esq. M. P. Lombard-street, London. Deal, September 27, 1828. Mr. Robarts, in consequence of the decision you have come to not to allow us any more discounts, I have looked into my affairs, and finding them rather deranged, I have concluded to be out of the way for a short time. I am yours, &c. J. HUNTON.
EDWARD HUNT. I am a Police-officer of Portsmouth. On the 7th of October I went on board a vessel called the Leeds, which lay at St. Helens, bound for America, New York; Forrester, a City officer, and Mr. Garnet, Mr. Gates' clerk, accompanied me; I found the prisoner in the cabin; I had taken a letter from Portsmouth which I showed to him - I did not deliver it to him; I asked him if his name was not Wilkinson, for I had a letter for him - he said it was Wilkinson; I then told him I was a Police-officer, and arrested him on a charge of forgery - there were several papers lying before him - he was in the act of writing at the time I addressed him; five or six letters were before him; I said, "Are these your letters;" he said they were; I then said, "I must take them into my possession;" I took them, put them into my pocket, and afterwards marked them (looking at document No. 2) - this is one of them; he was taken into custody and taken on shore.
The document No. 2 was here read, as follows:
To the Editor of the Times.
Sir, - The account published in your paper of last Saturday, relative to Joseph Hunton, being incorrect in almost every particular, I beg leave to send you this statement, which, be assured, is strictly accurate. It is not true, as stated, that he was seen in London by several persons, on Saturday, the 27th of September; he left London on Friday afternoon, about four o'clock, and has not been there since. It is not true that he has a family of twelve or thirteen children - he has but ten. It is not true that he resided chiefly in the country; he regularly came to town soon after nine o'clock in the morning, and generally returned about nine o'clock in the evening. It is not true that he has forged the names of several eminent mercantile houses in the city; there is not a single one of that description. It is not true that he has committed forgeries to the amount of 12,000l. or 14,000l. - the amount is about 5,000l., and these he has paid when due with interest, till, for want of discounts, he was no longer able to do so. The whole of his property, of every description, is left for the creditors, as, excepting a very small supply of clothing absolutely needful, and his passage, he did not take away to the amount of 5s., except what was very kindly advanced him by a relative, expressly that he may not be utterly destitute in a foreign country. It is true that he might have obtained goods on credit to a much larger amount, but this was not his object; he was under engagement to supply the capital for his concerns, and being very much disappointed in the quarter from whence he depended for a supply, he had unfortunate recourse to this ruinous measure. He is a very melancholy instance of the mutability of wealth. Several years since, he was retiring from business, with an ample fortune, but unfortunately investing a considerable part of his property in the foreign funds, he lost to a very great amount, and the more he struggled to extricate himself the more he lost; and the greater his difficulties increased. Those who are acquainted with him know that his habits and personal expenditure were on the most moderate scale, and were it not for the law expences and destruction of property attendant on a Commission of Bankruptcy, there will be sufficient to pay the whole of his creditors in full. It is not intended to apologise for his late practices, but a desire that his fate may be a warning to others to avoid them, and not to engage in any concern of which they do not foresee the consequence; he expected to have been able very shortly to pay these acceptances out of the regular proceeds of his concerns, this disappointment has brought on himself, and on a most amiable family, irretrievable ruin and disgrace.
The bill was then read, as sent out in the indictment.
The prisoner, in his Defence, read as follows:
My Lord, I beg permission to state respectfully to the Court, that I am wholly unprepared to take my trial now; I lately applied to the Court for the postponement of my trial untill the next Session, upon grounds which I then thought, and still venture to think, were, with reference to the justice of the case, sufficient to warrant that application: your Lordship was pleased to think that I had not established legal grounds for the postponement required, looking at the case merely in a technical point of view - of the impartiality of your Lordship's judgment I am fully satisfied - of its correctness as a mere dicision of law I entertain no doubt; but I can as little doubt, that if my prosecutors had consented to the application, the Court would have granted it; they, however, with an impetuosity of proceeding for which I can assign no adequate motive, arising from the mere consideration of public good, opposed the application: how public justice can be advanced by my being tried now, when I have not any means whatever of defence, instead of being tried at the next Session, when, by the inspection of my papers and books, I shall be better prepared for so awful a trial, I cannot see. I have only to say that, stripped as I am of all means of defence, by being denied access to my papers (which my assignees have promised to afford me, if the trial be postponed), I shall not do either myself or my counsel the injustice of attempting a defence, which must, of necessity, from the circumstances in which I am placed, be imperfect and illusory; I shall, therefore, attempt no defence, under such circumstances, I shall not trouble my counsel to make an useless interference; I shall cross-examine no witness, nor at all interfere in the trial, which my prosecutors may have all their own way, and conduct it as they may think fit; I can submit to become their victim, but I cannot submit to exhibit the appearance of defence, while they exclude me from the means of rendering it effectual. The prisoner then added, "I make no remarks on the extraordinary conduct of my prosecutors, in taking papers from my private desk, during my absence; they have deprived me of several of them which are material to my defence. I would further remark that several of them were not finished, nor were they intended to be sent to any body, but merely written for my own amusement, and would probably, in a few minutes, have been destroyed."
COURT, Q. Would you wish to call any witnesses? Prisoner. No, I adhere to my determination.
Q. Do you wish to call witnesses to character? A. I beg leave respectfully to state that I adhere to my former determination.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 58.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury on account of his large family and former respectability.
The Court fixed Tuesday, the 4th of November, for the trial of the further charges against the prisoner.

Before Mr. Justice Park.
2203. JOSEPH HUNTON was again indicted, for that he, on the 1st of September, at St. Mary-le-Bow, having in his custody and possession a certain bill of exchange which is as follows: -
No. 307, £94.13s, London, 23 August, 1828,
Four Months after date pay to our Order - Ninety-four Pounds, Thirteen Shillings, value received.
JOHN DICKSON and Co.
To Richard Luck, East Peckham, Kent. feloniously did falsely make, forge, and counterfeit, on the said bill of exchange, an acceptance of the same, which is as follows: -
Accepted and payable at the Spur Inn, Borough. R. LUCK. with intent to defraud Sir William Curtis, Bart, and others; against the Statute, &c.
2d COUNT, that he, on the same day, at the same parish, having in his custody and possession a certain bill of exchange which is as follows (Bill set out as above) - upon which said last-mentioned bill of exchange was written a certain false, forged, and counterfeited acceptance of the same, which is as follows (Acceptance set out as above) - he, the said Joseph Hunton, afterwards(to wit) on the same day, at the same parish, feloniously did utter, and publish as true, the said last-mentioned false, forged, and counterfeited acceptance of the said last-mentioned bill of exchange, he well knowing the same to be false, forged, and counterfeited, with intent to defraud the said Sir William Curtis, Bart. and others; against the Statute, &c.
3d COUNT, the same as the second; only for offering, disposing of, and putting away the said forged acceptance, instead of for uttering and publishing as true.
4th, 5th, and 6th COUNTS, the same as the three former, only charging the prisoner's intention to be to defraud John Dickson.
7th, 8th and 9th COUNTS, the same, only with intent to defraud Richard Luck, the younger.
MESSRS. LAW and CLAKKSON conducted the prosecution.
ABRAHAM WILDEY ROBARTS, ESQ. I am a partner in the house of Sir William Curtis and Co., bankers, of Lombard-street, City; the firm consists of Sir William Curtis, his son, and myself. In the course of last year and the present, the house of Dickson and Co. were customers of ours- I believe they resided in Ironmonger-lane; the firm consisted of the prisoner and John Dickson - the firm stood in our books in the name of John Dickson and Co.; we were in the habit of frequently receiving bills from them, for the purpose of being discounted; the prisoner exclusively carried on the financial department of the firm, at least as far as we were concerned; he frequently came to our banking-house with bills. On the 1st of September, he brought several bills, and brought this list with them - (looking at it) - there is some cash entered at the bottom of the list, but that did not come into my hands - this is a list of bills which were offered for discount; it is in the prisoner's handwriting - I saw him myself that day; I looked at the total amount at the bottom of the list, and ran over the bills - they corresponded.
Q. You have a bill of exchange in your hand, was that among the number of bills brought by the prisoner on the 1st of September? A. It was, and it corresponds with the entry on the list - it agrees in the sum, the names of the drawer and acceptor, and where payable.
Q. Did you say any thing to the prisoner on that occasion? A. I can only recollect observing to the prisoner on that occasion, as well as on every other, when he brought bills to be discounted; I always expressed my very great objection to discount them - I perfectly recollect objecting to discount them on this particular occasion; he invariably made a reply, and on this occasion he told me, distinctly, that they were all bona fide legitimate commercial transactions, and upon that representation I discounted them; after having received them from him, I wrapped them up in this list, and took them into the discount-office, and put them into a box there - they would then be taken from that box, and entered by Mr. Thomas Brackett, the discount clerk, in a book kept exclusively for that purpose; it was then the practice for Mr. Brackett to put them under a lead on his desk - they would then be taken from there by Mr. Clunie, another clerk, who keeps the bill-book.
Q. At any time after the 1st of September did you decline to discount any further for the firm of Dickson and Co.? A. Certainly; it was either on the Wednesday or Thursday prior to the date on which I received a letter from him - this is the letter (looking at it); I had told him distinctly I would discount no more for him; this letter is in the prisoner's hand-writing, and bears the Deal post-mark - it is dated the 27th of September, 1828; I received it on the Monday morning - I am certain it was on the Mondayfollowing the day I had declined discounting further for them; it was addressed to A. W. Robarts, Esq., M.P., 15. Lombard-street, London - it is above number, and of course the postage is charged [the letter being read was the same as was given in evidence on the former case, Page 995] - previous to the receipt of this letter I found, on coming to town on that Monday morning, his partner, Mr. Dickson, at our house - I immediately looked out the different bills, that they might be submitted to his inspection.
COURT. Q. Do you happen to known in whose handwriting the body of this bill is? A. I do; it is the prisoner's - and the signature, J. Dickson and Co. is his; I have no knowledge whatever of R. Luck.
THOMAS BRACKETT. I am clerk to Sir Wm. Curtis and Co. bankers, of Lombard-street. My department is in the discount-office - on the 1st of September I took from the box in the discount-office the bills and papers, and entered the bills in the discount book (producing it) - I found this list of bills in the box; I have written on it "1st Sept. Bills" - that is my hand-writing; I accurately entered all these bills in the discount-book - the entry corresponds with the particulars of the bills in this list - they are placed to the credit of John Dickson and Co. in this book; and at the same time I debited them for the discount - among the bills entered is one corresponding with the one produced(reads) "1st Sept. J. Dickson and Co. 94l. 13. on Luck, due 26th December" - that entry corresponds both with the bill and the list; the discount is charged for the whole amount of bills together - it was Mr. Clunie's business to enter the bills in the bill-book, and for that purpose I place them on the desk under a lead.
The item from the list being read was as follows: "Dickson and Co. Luck, Spur Inn, 26 Dec. 94l. 13s." the total amount of bills was £1636 3s. 2d.
ALEXANDER CLUNIE. I am a clerk in the banking-house of Sir William Curtis and Co. It was my duty in September last to enter in the bill-book bills which had been discounted, and had gone through Mr. Brackett's hands - on the 1st of September I took a number of bills from his desk, in the regular course of business; I have not entered the gross amount of the bills in the name of J. Dickson and Co. - I have an entry on that day, in my own hand-writing, of the bills corresponding with this list; the entry was made at the time, and is headed "J. Dickson and Co., September 1st (reads) "Luck, August 23d, four months, due December 26th, 94l. 13s.," and the letter D is put against it for discount; and on the bill itself I have written D 589," meaning the folio in book D where it is entered; the entry on the bill, and in the book were made on the same day, on the same occasion - I have no recollection of this identical bill, except from this entry; there is no other bill of the same amount, or at all corresponding with this entry - that enables me to say it is one of the bills I entered in the book that day.
JOHN DICKSON. I am a haberdasher and warehouseman, and live in Ironmonger-lane, in the City. On the 1st of September, and previous to that time, I was in partnership with the prisoner - he managed the money concerns of the house.
Q. Look at this bill, and state in whose hand-writing the body and drawing of it is A. The prisoner's.
Q. Was the bill-book kept by the prisoner? A. We had a book called a bill-receivable book, into which all regular trade bills were entered - I have examined that book, to ascertain whether this bill is entered; there is no memorandum of it in that book - I have examined it attentively, and think I can swear no such bill is entered; I have not the book here - we had a correspondent named Richard or Robert Luck; he resided at East Peckam, Kent - he was indebted to us, but I think not to the amount of this bill - the list of bills produced is in the prisoner's hand-writing; I have no doubt of it.
COURT Q. Are you acquainted with Luck's handwriting. A. No; I do not know whose hand-writing the acceptance of the bill is.
RICHARD LUCK. I live at East Peckham, Kent; I am in the general line, a grocer and draper - I have lived there some time; I do not know of any other Richard Luck at East Peckham - I am twenty-three years of age - I served my apprenticeship there, and have lived there ever since my apprenticeship commenced, except for about fifteen months; I know no other Richard Luck in the parish, nor at East Peckham - in the adjoining parish there is a Mr. Luck, but I do not believe his name is Richard - he lives about two miles and a half from me, and is in the general line; I deal with Dickson and Co., and did so in September last - I am in their debt; I do not know to what amount, but it is not to the amount of this bill; the acceptance to this bill is not my handwriting (looking at it), nor did I authorize any one to sign it - I have given Dickson and Co. bills to the amount I am indebted to them; I gave them one to the amount of 19l. odd, and had given them one before for 12l. odd - both these were given before the 1st of September, and I believe were to the full amount I owed them; I am certain I never gave them the bill in question.
COURT Q. What is the name of the parish in which the other Luck lives? A. Yalding, and there is a Luck at Teaton, which is four miles from here; I believe he is in the general line as well - he is father of the Luck of Yalding.
JAMES WELLS. I keep an ale-house at East Peckham, and know the last witness; I know his hand-writing, and have seen him write for the last six or seven years (looking at the bill) - I believe this acceptance not be his handwriting; there is no other Richard Luck at East Peckham; I know Luck of Yalding - his Christian name is Thomas, I think, but am not quite sure.
JOSEPH WARREN. I live at Bury St. Edmunds, and am a draper. I was formerly apprenticed there to the prisoner - I was about six years with him, and am well acquainted with his hand-writing.
Q. Look at this bill, and tell the Court in whose handwriting you believe the acceptance to be, and the name of R. Luck particularly? A. I believe it to be the prisoner's hand-writing - the words "Spur Inn" more particularly; but I believe the whole to be his writing; I believe the"R. Luck" to be so - it says
Accepted, payable at the Spur Inn, Borough. R. LUCK. I believe the whole of that to be the prisoner's handwriting.
MR. LAW. Q. Look at this paper (the list of bills) in whose hand-writing do you believe that to be? A. The prisoner's; also this (the letter to Mr. Robarts) - this is [Pages missing in original.]

Before Mr. Justice Park.
JOSEPH HUNTON was indicted for forging, and uttering a bill of exchange, knowing the same to be forged; he was tried on a subsequent day on another indictment; the report of both cases will be found after the proceedings of the New Court.
